      Severe
    Headline
    laboratory test interference
    General Information
    Use of segesterone acetate; ethinyl estradiol, as with other contraceptive steroids, may result in clinical changes that influence the results of certain laboratory tests, such as coagulation factors, lipids, glucose tolerance, and binding proteins. Specific laboratory test interference has not been reported.

Description
  Text
  Segesterone acetate and ethinyl estradiol and used together in a combination hormonal contraceptive (CHC) vaginal system (ring) for routine contraception. Ethinyl estradiol is a potent, synthetic estrogen. Segesterone, also known as 17alpha-hydroxy-16-methylene-19-norprogesterone or as 17alpha-deacetylnestorone, is a synthetic progestin. Segesterone has high progestational activity with negligible androgenic, glucocorticoid, or estrogenic activity in vivo. This contraceptive vaginal ring is inserted for 3 weeks and then removed for a 1-week dose-free interval. Following the dose-free interval, the ring is reinserted for another 21 continuous days (3 complete weeks). This cyclic pattern of use may be repeated for up to 13 cycles (1 year) with a single segesterone; ethinyl estradiol vaginal ring device. There are no epidemiologic data available to determine if vaginal CHC ring safety profile is different than other CHCs, and the standard risks and benefits of the CHCs apply. CHCs can be used in female patients from menarche to over the age of 40 years up until the time of menopause with proper selection of products, if no contraindications exist. The choice of a routine contraceptive for any given patient is based on the individual's contraceptive needs, underlying medical conditions or risk factors for adverse effects, and individual preferences for use. All CHCs have risks related to venous and arterial thromboembolism; CHC labels contain a boxed warning regarding the increased risk for thromboembolism in tobacco smokers. The Centers for Disease Control's U.S. Medical Eligibility Criteria describes considerations for risk vs. benefits, including medical conditions or attributes that contraindicate use; these criteria can help practitioners determine product selection for individual patients. Segesterone acetate; ethinyl estradiol was FDA approved in August 2018.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  The primary action of the combination of an estrogen with a progestin is to suppress the hypothalamic-pituitary system, decreasing the secretion of gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH). Progestins blunt luteinizing hormone (LH) release, and estrogens suppress follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) from the anterior pituitary. Both estrogen and progestin ultimately inhibit maturation and release of the dominant ovule. In addition, viscosity of the cervical mucus increases with hormonal contraceptive use, which increases the difficulty of sperm entry into the uterus. Alteration in endometrial tissues also occurs, which reduces the likelihood of implantation of the fertilized ovum. The contraceptive effect is reversible. When traditional regimens of oral contraception are discontinued, ovulation usually returns within three menstrual cycles but can take up to 6 months in some women. Pituitary function and ovarian functions recover more quickly than endometrial activity, which can take up to 3 months to regain normal histology.
 
Both estrogens and progestins are responsible for a number of other metabolic changes. The summary of these changes is dependent on the net actions of the estrogen and progestin combinations. At the cellular level, estrogens and progestins diffuse into their target cells and interact with a protein receptor. Metabolic responses to estrogens and progestins require an interaction between DNA and the hormone-receptor complex. Target cells include the female reproductive tract, the mammary gland, the hypothalamus, and the pituitary. Estrogens increase the hepatic synthesis of sex hormone binding globulin (SHBG), thyroid-binding globulin (TBG), and other serum proteins. Estrogens generally have a favorable effect on blood lipids, reducing LDL and increasing HDL cholesterol concentrations. Serum triglycerides increase with estrogen administration. Folate metabolism and excretion is increased by estrogens and may lead to slight serum folate deficiency. Estrogens also enhance sodium and fluid retention. Progestins are classified according to their progestational, estrogenic and androgenic properties. Progestins can alter hepatic carbohydrate metabolism, increase insulin resistance, and have either little to slightly favorable effects on serum lipoproteins. Less androgenic progestins, like segesterone, have only slight effects on carbohydrate metabolism and are less likely to aggravate acne or produce other androgenic effects. Serious adverse events, like thrombosis, are primarily associated with the estrogen component of hormonal contraceptives but may be the result of both estrogen and progestin components. The mechanism for thrombosis may be associated with increased clotting factor production and/or decreases in anti-thrombin III. Minor side effects can be addressed by choosing formulations that take advantage of relative estrogen, progestin, and androgenic potencies.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Other Route(s)
    Description
    Vaginal route
Following vaginal administration, segesterone acetate and ethinyl estradiol were absorbed into systemic circulation with a median time to maximal concentration (Tmax) of about 2 hours in cycle 1, cycle 3, and cycle 13. Concentrations of both hormones declined after Tmax and became more constant after 96 hours post-dose. Over subsequent cycles of use, the peak serum concentrations and AUCs of both hormones declined. In cycle 1, the mean Cmax (SD) for segesterone acetate was 1.147 (315) pg/mL and for ethinyl estradiol was 129 (39) pg/mL, respectively. At cycle 13 (1 year of use), the Cmax (SD) for segesterone acetate was 294 (116) pg/mL and for ethinyl estradiol was 39 (16) pg/mL, respectively. In cycle 1, the AUC 0-21 day (SD) for segesterone acetate was 96.2 (16.9) ng x hour/mL and for ethinyl estradiol was 22.2 (9.8) ng x hour/mL, respectively. In cycle 13 (1 year of use), the AUC (SD) for segesterone acetate was 47.2 (10.1) ng x hour/mL and for ethinyl estradiol was 9.6 (4.1) ng x hour/mL, respectively.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    No studies have been conducted to evaluate the effect of hepatic impairment on the disposition of segesterone acetate; ethinyl estradiol vaginal ring. However, steroid hormones may be poorly metabolized in patients with hepatic impairment, and are contraindicated in patients with hepatic disease such as acute hepatitis or severe or severe (decompensated) cirrhosis.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    No studies of segesterone acetate; ethinyl estradiol vaginal ring were conducted in subjects with renal impairment.
    Name
    Obesity
    Description
    Patients with a higher body weight have lower systemic exposure of segesterone acetate and ethinyl estradiol. In a pharmacokinetic study of 18 females with BMI less than 25 (range, 16.89 to 24.34) kg/m2 and 21 females with BMI greater than 25 (range, 5.15 to 37.46) kg/m2, up to 16% and 33% decreases in the systemic exposure (AUC 0-21 day) of segesterone acetate and ethinyl estradiol, respectively, were observed between the 2 BMI groups. The efficacy of the segesterone acetate; ethinyl estradiol ring has not been adequately studied in females with a BMI more than 29 kg/m2.
  Text
  Segesterone acetate; ethinyl estradiol is administered by the vaginal route. Each vaginal ring slowly releases 0.15 mg/day of segesterone acetate and 0.013 mg/day of ethinyl estradiol over a 3-week period of use. Both hormones are widely distributed after absorption. Ethinyl estradiol (EE) is highly but non-specifically protein-bound to albumin (98.5%); EE induces an increase in the serum concentrations of sex hormone-binding globulin (SHBG). Segesterone acetate is roughly 95% bound to human serum proteins and has negligible binding affinity for sex hormone-binding globulin (SHBG). In vitro studies show that both segesterone acetate and EE are metabolized by the CYP3A4 isoenzyme. Ethinyl estradiol is primarily metabolized by hydroxylation but a wide variety of hydroxylated and methylated metabolites are formed. These are present as free metabolites and as sulfate and glucuronide conjugates. The hydroxylated EE metabolites have weak estrogenic activity. In human serum, 2 oxidative metabolites constitute 50% of exposure relative to segesterone acetate; both metabolites are considered inactive. Excretion of the hormonal steroids occurs via the urine, bile and feces. EE is known to be excreted in the urine and feces as glucuronide and sulfate conjugates, and it undergoes enterohepatic recirculation. The mean (SD) elimination half-life is roughly 4.5 (3.4) hours for segesterone acetate and 15.1 (7.5) hours for EE.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP3A4, CYP2B6, CYP2C19, CYP2C9, P-glycoprotein (P-gp)
Ethinyl estradiol: In vitro and in vivo studies indicate that estrogens such as ethinyl estradiol are partially metabolized by CYP3A4. Interactions with drugs that are inhibitors or inducers of CYP3A4 are possible. Ethinyl estradiol is a substrate of the drug transporter P-gp. While ethinyl estradiol is an in vitro inhibitor of CYP2B6, 2C19, and 3A4 and the drug is an in vitro substrate of 2C9, clinically significant drug interactions are not expected through these pathways.
Segesterone acetate: In vitro data show that segesterone acetate is metabolized by CYP3A4. Segesterone acetate is unlikely to inhibit or induce CYP enzymes at the therapeutic dose.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
 
Hazardous Drugs Classification
NIOSH 2024 List: Table 1
Observe and exercise appropriate precautions for handling, preparation, administration, and disposal of hazardous drugs.
Use double chemotherapy gloves and a protective gown. Eye/face and respiratory protection may be needed during preparation and administration.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Name
    Intravaginal Administration
    Description
    Vaginal Ring Contraceptive (i.e., Annovera)
For vaginal use only.
Inform the patient regarding the risks and benefits of combined hormonal contraceptives.
The vaginal system may be reused for a total of 13 cycles (up to 1 year). After this time, the ring must be discarded and replaced with a new system.
Educate the patient regarding proper insertion and removal of the ring. See "Instructions for Use".
Insertion:
The ring will be inserted for 21 days (3 weeks) of each cycle. Advise the patient not to deviate from the recommended regimen. The day and time of insertion should be noted so that the ring remains in place continuously for 3 weeks and can be removed 3 weeks later on the same day and at about the same time.
When properly inserted, the ring should be entirely in the vagina and behind the pelvic bone; however, the exact position of the ring inside the vagina is not critical for its function.
The user can choose the insertion position that is most comfortable for her.
Avoid the use of vaginal products such as oil-based suppositories, oil-based creams, or oil-based gels while the vaginal system is in place. Do not use silicone- or oil-containing vaginal lubricants. Water-based lubricants are okay.
Removal:
The ring is removed for a 1-week dose-free interval during each cycle, during which a withdrawal bleed usually occurs within 2 to 3 days. The ring can be removed by hooking an index finger into the vaginal system inside the vagina and gently pulling the ring. If the ring has been left in place for more than 21 days, it should be removed for 7 days and then reinserted for 21 days to resume a 21/7 schedule.
Cleaning and Storage during 1-week dose-free interval: The removed ring should be cleaned with mild soap and warm water, patted dry with a clean cloth towel or paper towel, and placed in its case during the 1-week dose-free interval. At the end of the dose-free interval, the ring should be cleaned before being placed back in the vagina for another 21 continuous days (3 complete weeks).
Reinsertion: The ring is inserted 1 week after the last ring was removed, on the same day and at about the same time. The ring should be inserted 1 week later even if withdrawal bleeding has not stopped.
Disposition following 13 cycles (1 year) of use: Once removed, the used ring should be placed in the compact case and discarded via a drug take-back option if one is available. If a take-back option is unavailable, then discard in the waste receptacle out of reach of children and pets. The ring should NOT be flushed down the toilet. See www.fda.gov/drugdisposal for more information about disposal of medicines.
 
Expelled or Lost Ring, Missed Reinsertion, or Prolonged System-free interval
If the ring is expelled and is left outside of the vagina for less than 2 hours, contraceptive efficacy is not reduced. The ring should be washed with mild soap and warm water, rinsed and patted dry with a clean cloth towel or paper towel, and reinserted as soon as possible, but at the latest, within 2 hours.
If the ring is expelled and left out of the vagina for more than 2 continuous hours or more than 2 cumulative hours (multiple inadvertent removals or expulsions adding up to 2 hours), then back-up contraception, such as male condoms or spermicide, should be used until the ring has been used continuously for 7 days. The use of combined hormonal contraceptives (those containing an estrogen) for emergency contraception during use of the ring is not recommended.
If the ring-free interval is prolonged, consider the possibility of pregnancy and have the woman use back-up contraception, such as condoms or spermicides until the new ring has been used continuously for 7 days. The use of combined hormonal contraceptives (those containing an estrogen) for emergency contraception during use of the ring is not recommended.
 
General information when initiating routine contraception with CHCs: 66717
Initiating CHCs in an individual not previously taking a CHC: There are options to start using the first cycle of the CHC.
Day 1 start: Patient will start using the CHC during the first 24 hours of their menstrual period. The patient will not need to use a back-up method of birth control. If the CHC is initiated after the first 24 hours of menstruation, non-hormonal back-up contraception (such as condoms and spermicide, or diaphragm and spermicide) is needed for the first 7 days of the first cycle only.
Sunday start: Patient will start using the CHC on the first Sunday after the onset of their menstrual period. When initiating a Sunday start regimen, another method of contraception, such as condoms or spermicide, should be used for the first 7 consecutive days of CHC use.
Quick-start: Alternatively, a CHC may be initiated at any time during the menstrual cycle provided the individual is not pregnant. Another method of contraception, such as condoms or spermicide, should be used for the first 7 consecutive days of CHC use unless the individual abstains from sexual intercourse or the CHC is initiated in the first 5 days of menstrual bleeding.
Switching from a different CHC or other contraceptive methods:
When switching from another combined hormonal contraceptive, start the CHC regimen on the same day that a new pack of the previous oral contraceptive would have been started.
Otherwise, CHCs can usually be started immediately if it is reasonably certain that the person is not pregnant. Waiting for their next menstrual period is unnecessary. If it has been more than 5 days since menstrual bleeding started, the individual needs to abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days. Consult specialized recommendations if the patient is switching from an IUD to a CHC.
Initiating a CHC in the person with amenorrhea:
CHCs can be started at any time if it is reasonably certain that the individual is not pregnant. Have the patient abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days.
Initiating CHCs postpartum:
In general, do not initiate in the first 3 weeks postpartum due to thromboembolic risk. If the individual is breast-feeding, do not initiate until after the fourth week postpartum to avoid interference with lactation. Individuals with other risk factors for venous thromboembolism generally should not use CHCs for 3 to 6 weeks (4 to 6 weeks if breast-feeding) after delivery. If the individual starts on a COC postpartum and is less than 6 months postpartum, amenorrheic, and fully or nearly fully breastfeeding (exclusively breastfeeding or the vast majority [85% or more] of feeds are breastfeeds), no additional contraceptive protection is needed. Otherwise, an individual who is 21 days or more days postpartum and has not experienced return of their menstrual cycle needs to abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days. If menstrual cycles have returned, and it has been more than 5 days since menstrual bleeding started, they need to abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days.
Initiating CHCs following induced or spontaneous abortion:
CHCs may be started at any time postabortion, including immediately after abortion completion or at the time of medication abortion initiation. The patient needs to abstain from sexual intercourse or use an additional contraceptive method for the next 7 days unless the CHC is started at the time of the abortion.

  Age Groups

    Name

    Max Dosage

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Combined hormonal contraceptives are contraindicated for use in the presence of active liver disease (e.g., acute hepatitis or severe /decompensated cirrhosis) or markedly impaired liver function.
  Renal Impairment
  Segesterone acetate; ethinyl estradiol vaginal ring is not recommended for use in patients with renal impairment. No studies were conducted in women with renal impairment.
